The button and the status-bar context count turn orange above *Suggest compacting above* (global, default 200,000 tokens; per-project override; **Never** turns it off). The Assistant has its own threshold, **Settings → Assistant → Highlight Compact over** (`settings.assistant.compactSuggestTokens`, default 500,000, **Never** = 0; it usually runs a bigger context): `assistantProjectConfig()` overlays it as its host's `compactSuggestTokens`, so its header's Compact (highlighted, with the same "Recommended…" tooltip) and its footer's context (amber, "— consider compacting", the `compact-suggested` tip) resolve it through the same `compactThreshold()` as agents' (#290). Until then its footer used the agents' 200,000 and its Compact button was never highlighted. The full pre-compaction history stays in the transcript and Hive's backup. |
| Archive | Copies the current transcript to `.hive/archive/` (or moves the backup there if Claude Code no longer has it), removes the active backup and marks the session archived. Can be unarchived. Only Hive's sessions (adopt an external one first), and only when it isn't in use (as Delete). |
| In use (Archive, Delete) | Archive and Delete first **reserve** the session (as Clean Up does): until they finish, it can't start or resume and its transcript isn't read (the viewer, a search, an export wait). Then Hive checks nothing has it in use (`sessions.inUse`), and refuses (one at a time) or skips it (Archive All / Delete All, saying why): it is **running or starting** in any agent, project or window; Hive is **reading its transcript** (a read under way: `transcriptReads.ts`, counted around every parse, export and image); its transcript is **open in a Sessions view**, in any window (views register with `transcript:viewing`, and can't register on a reserved session; the tab asking closes its own view first and waits for main to know, opens it again afterwards if the session is still there (skipped, or archived one at a time), and says when it stays closed); **another program holds the CLI's transcript open** (found by opening it to read and closing it at once: the CLI's file is never changed or moved); or its CLI is **still writing** it: changed in the last 5 s and not since Hive saw that CLI exit (Hive notes each exit of its own sessions, so Archive and Start New archives one just stopped; a session started outside Hive, adopted or not, or a Codex guardian review still going, waits). Nothing is ever killed. **All or nothing**: an archive builds the new archive aside (the backup brought up to date from the CLI's transcript), then moves each copy, and any failure (a copy another program holds, the record not saved) moves every copy back; the record changes only after the files. A delete first moves each of Hive's copies aside and back, keeps a spare of each (a hard link, else a copy), then sends them to the Recycle Bin and removes the record; if one fails to go or the record can't be saved, the copies already gone are put back from their spares (a copy stays in the Recycle Bin). |
| Delete | Any session that isn't running (Hive's, archived or external), from the Sessions tab or the Assistant's conversations, after asking. Hive's backup and archive copies go to the Recycle Bin, the record leaves `sessions.json` and its id is kept in `deleted` so it never shows again (not even as external); **what it used is kept** (`deletedUsage`: its usage with the day buckets, no transcript), so the Overview's and the Assistant's totals still count it (`session:keptUsage`; lists, search and resume never see it); an agent whose last session it was forgets it. The CLI's own transcript is left alone: Hive doesn't change the CLIs' files, so the CLI's own resume list still has it. If it runs again (resumed inside the CLI), it is Hive's again (and its kept usage goes, counted from its transcript instead). Images pasted into it stay in `.hive/images`. |
| Adopt | Sessions for the project started outside Hive are listed and can be adopted into `sessions.json`. A sub-session stays one when adopted (its `sub` is kept): never resumed. |
| Add agent | Up to twelve per project (Agents per project, #562: up to 24), a page holding as many as the layout has panes (§6.2). **Add Agent** is a split button (#286): the main part is **Configure Agent and Add…**, which opens the Add Agent… dialog; **▾** is a menu with Configure Agent and Add… and, in a project (not the Assistant), **Add Agent from Template** (the templates' agents, § Agent templates). The quick add (the default provider with its default settings, in the project folder, named "Agent n") is the palette's **Add Agent** (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N), the project's right-click menu and New Session in a project without agents. The dialog: provider, name, where it works (project folder, new worktree with branch `hive/<workspace>/<project>/<name>` (#703) from a chosen base branch, with the hint "`hive/` marks branches Hive made, so it can reuse and tidy them up" under it (#702), or an existing unused worktree of the project), optional model/effort/permission overrides (and Use 200K context for Claude Code), and "Start a session now". A page whose layout wasn't chosen by hand shows all its agents (1 one at a time, 2 two columns, 3 three columns, 4 the 2×2 grid, 5–6 the 3×2 grid), and the new agent is focused (the seventh opens page 2). Adding the seventh shows a note that each agent is its own CLI process (roughly 200–500 MB of memory; measured on Darren's machine, 13 Oct: Claude Code about 410 MB working set, Codex 210 MB, Copilot 180 MB) and they share the plan's limits. |
| Agent names (#702) | **Unique across the workspace** (Darren's decision of 12 Oct), case and spacing aside: two projects' B1 couldn't be told apart on the board, in notifications, in Apiary's office or by the Assistant. Refused, never only warned: Add Agent and quick add (the default "Agent n" numbered past every project's names), a rename (Agent Settings, the Agent API's update), a template's load and Add Agent from Template, and a card started with a new agent; the dialogs say so inline before Save or Add ("alpha already has an agent called B1… Use HB1", `shared/agentNames.ts`), and main checks again. **Suggestion:** the project's initials (`projectPrefix`: hive → H, hive-desktop → HD) in front of a short code, in capitals (B1 → HB1, then longer prefixes AMB1…), a word with the project after it ("Builder (beta)"), else numbered. **Race-free:** every change that gives a name checks it and writes it holding the workspace's names lock (`withAgentNames` in `main/agentNames.ts`, taken before a project file's lock, never inside one), so two projects can't take a name at once; the listed projects count (a hidden one's agents are flagged if they clash when it comes back). **Names from before** keep working: saving an agent's other settings with its own name isn't a rename; each name two projects share is said once per workspace (a warning with **Rename <project>'s <name>…**, which opens Agent Settings through the `agent.settings` command), remembered in workspace.json's `flaggedAgentNames` while it lasts. The Assistant's name isn't checked: it is never a project's agent. |
| Merge (worktree agent) | Commits the worktree's uncommitted changes (with the message given), checks for conflicts with `git merge-tree` without touching anything, then merges into the branch checked out in the project folder: **squash** (one commit, default) or **merge** (`--no-ff`). Refused while the project folder has staged changes, and while the agent is in the middle of a task (starting, working, waiting for the user's answer, or waiting on background tasks it started: its uncommitted files would be committed half-done; `mergeBlocked()`): Merge… in the agent header and ⋯ menu, the Agents settings and the Changes tab are disabled then, with the reason in the tooltip (the unmerged count still shows), and an open Merge dialog says so and disables Merge. On conflict nothing changes; the dialog lists the files and offers to send merge instructions to an idle agent in the project folder, or copy them. Optionally removes the worktree, branch and agent afterwards (off by default; agent must be stopped). After a **squash** that keeps the worktree, **Move the branch afterwards** (on by default) runs `git reset --keep <into>` in the worktree, only when it is on the agent's branch and merging the branch again would change nothing (`git merge-tree`); otherwise the merge still succeeds and a warning says why the branch wasn't moved (`MergeResult.branchMoved`/`moveError`). **Unmerged work** shows on Merge… in the agent header (accent colour, with the count of commits not in the project folder's branch, or • for uncommitted files only; a branch whose merge would change nothing — its commits were squash-merged already — counts as merged, checked with `git merge-tree`) and on the agent's strip tab (↑n or •), with the details in their tooltips. `main/branchWatch.ts` asks git (`branchStatus()`) when the agent's status changes to anything but working or starting, when a window gets focus (at most every 10 s), for agents not checked yet (on `workspace-changed`), for every worktree agent of the project after a merge, when the Merge dialog checks it, and every 60 s for agents with something left to merge; changes go to the window as `branch-status` events (not on the Agent API's event stream). |
| Merge slot (#350) | One merge at a time into a project's branch, so it can't move while an agent checks and merges (#306 made merges check that the base hadn't moved; with several lanes finishing together that meant rerunning the checks each time). `main/mergeSlots.ts` (the logic, synchronous between awaits; one Slot object per project and branch stays registered while anything uses it, so a clean-up never leaves a claim holding an unregistered one) and `mergeSlotHost.ts` (wiring): a slot per project path and branch, in memory. **Holder:** a running agent's launch (project, agent, `runId`; from its own token, never the request), or the user's merge from the Merge dialog (`asUser`, for the length of `mergeWorktree`; refused while an agent holds or waits for it, and the dialog keeps Merge disabled with "B4 is merging #305 into main" until it is free). **Claim** (`hive_merge_slot`, `POST /v1/projects/{name}/merge-slot/claim`) waits in the call up to 240 s (at most 290: Node's `fetch` in the hive tools gives up on a reply after 300 s) and the line is first come, first served. A claim that times out, or whose client goes away, keeps its place for 2 minutes. A turn that comes while the waiter is between calls is given as not yet taken (`taken: false`) and lapses to the next in line after 2 minutes. Claiming while holding extends the hold. **Hold:** 60 minutes, kept while the holder's open Progress run reported in the last 15. It ends with the holder's launch (`sessions.onLaunchEnded`, and found out lazily from `runId`), on release, when the user releases it (Overview or Progress panel, after a confirm; the confirm names its hold by id, and is refused if the slot changed hands meanwhile, even to the same agent again), or on expiry (passed on, a warning toast, and the old holder is told on its next call: `lost`). **Shown:** the waiter's and holder's `mergeSlot` note on its status ("Waiting for the merge slot (B4 is merging #305)", "Merging into main (merge slot)"; `statusMessage` takes precedence), the project's Overview and the top of the Progress panel (its merge card, #484; `merge-slots-changed`). **Restart:** agents' holds are written to `merge-slots/` in app data (§4.3); when the workspace opens, a recorded hold no launch has is reported ("check the main checkout for a merge left half done") and dropped. Merges outside Hive aren't controlled; merge-ready keeps the "has the base moved?" check as the fallback. Agents only (not the Assistant): `AGENT_ONLY_TOOLS`. |
| Worktree branches (#703) | A new worktree's branch is **`hive/<workspace>/<project>/<agent>`**, always in full (Darren's decision on #702: several workspaces can share a repository, even with projects of the same name, and never clash), e.g. `hive/HIVE/amberlight/amb1` (`shared/hiveBranches.ts` `hiveBranch`). The workspace and project are their folder names with their case kept and git's ref rules applied (`refPart`: anything but letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `-` becomes `-`, no `..`, no leading or trailing `.` or `-`, no trailing `.lock`, at most 40 characters, `workspace`/`project` when nothing is left); the agent is its slug, as before. `uniqueBranch` numbers a taken one (`-2`), comparing case aside (git keeps branches as files on Windows), and never picks one git can't make: not one a branch is inside of (`hive/ws/alpha/x` while `hive/ws/alpha/x/y` exists), and one inside a branch (`feature/y` while `feature` exists) is refused, saying which. **A branch from before in the way** (round 2): an agent once called "ws" has `hive/ws`, and git can't make `hive/ws/<project>/…` while it exists (case aside: `hive/hive` blocks `hive/HIVE/…`); so the project's new branches take a numbered workspace part, `hive/ws-2/<project>/<agent>` (`chooseSpace`, also when `hive/<workspace>/<project>` is a branch), and the branch in the way is left as it is. **Which parts are the project's** (round 3) is recorded, never inferred from a name that looks numbered (another workspace may be called ws-2): project.json's `branchSpaces` gets a part when Hive first makes a branch in it, and only the workspace's own part and those count as the project's. A new part is never one another workspace uses in the repository (a branch under `hive/<part>/<project>/`): ws-3 then. **Stable:** once moved, the project stays in the part it moved to while that is clear, even after the branch in the way has gone; its own again only if that one is blocked. The Add Agent dialog gets the part and the project's parts from git info (`ProjectGitInfo.branchSpace`, `branchSpaces`) for its default and its look. **Branches from before** (`hive/<agent>`) stay Hive's: `hiveBranchNumber(branch, home, name)` says whether a branch is the one Hive made for an agent of that name, numbered or not, in either form: the old form compared with the slug exactly as Hive made it then (B--1 had `hive/b--1`; the new rules write its part `b-1`), the full form with the new parts, in one of the project's own parts and for this project only, so another workspace's same-named project's branches are never taken for this one's. For an agent's own default branch, Add Agent also looks for the same agent's branch in the project's other own parts (kept from before it moved), then its branch from before #703 (`reusableBranches`). Template reuse (`reusableWorktree`), unused-worktree clean-up after a template's deletion (`unusedWorktreesNamed`) and Add Agent's reuse of an unused worktree (`matchingWorktree` with `reusableBranches`, given the agent's name, also in the dialog's look, `agents:worktreeMatch`'s `name`) recognise both. The Add Agent dialog's Branch shows the full form, and Hive's tool `hive_add_agent` says it is the default. |
| Reusing a kept worktree (#456) | Adding an agent with a new worktree (the dialog, the Agent API) uses the project's unused worktree on the branch it would make (`hive/<workspace>/<project>/<name>`, or the same agent's `hive/<name>` from before #703 when the branch is that default: `reusableBranches`, the full form first; in Hive's worktrees folder) instead of `-2`, when it is merged into the base (a squash merge counting) and clean: a convenience, not policing. It is reused only when nothing uses it (Darren, 11 Oct: option A): no agent, not being removed, merged or taken by another add, no git lock on it (its `index.lock` and `HEAD.lock`, its branch's ref lock, `packed-refs.lock`: where git itself says they are) and no process working in it (Windows refuses to rename a folder a process has its working folder or a file open in, so Hive renames it aside and back). That is checked once, then `git reset --keep` brings it up to the base: a convenience, not a guard against work started in it in that moment. If anything uses it, or Hive can't tell, a new worktree is made, saying why, and the setup command runs again only when a lockfile changed. One holding work is left alone: the API makes a new one and says why, the dialog offers to reuse it with its work (Existing worktree). An existing worktree the user picks is brought up the same way when merged and clean, else taken as it is. The reply and a notice say which happened. Templates reuse their own way (#289). |
| Remove agent | Any agent, once stopped. A worktree agent asks whether to keep the worktree and branch (reusable via "existing worktree") or delete both. Later agents move up (page 2 empties first); automatic layouts follow, chosen ones stay. |
| Reorder agents | A project's agents have one order, saved in `project.json` (the `agents` array; added ones go last): the strip, its pages and the panes follow it, every window agrees, and the Agent API lists agents in it. Dragging an agent (its strip tab or its pane by the header, the same either way; #135): dropped on **another agent's pane** it **swaps** with it (each takes the other's place, across pages too; running or not, nothing restarts; `swapAgents`, saved under the project file's lock), the pane showing **Swap with <name>**; dropped on an **empty pane** (the last page's spare ones, **Move here**) it moves to the end; dropped **on the strip** it is inserted between tabs (a bar marks where it lands: the left half of a tab drops before it, the right half after), with no marker where it would stay where it is; there is no drop target where a drop would change nothing: its own pane, the empty panes when it is already last, and the button of the page whose last place it already holds (that button still shows its page when held over; #238). Held over another page's **button** for 600 ms, that page shows, so it can be dropped on one of its panes; dropped **on a page button** it moves to that page's last place. Escape or a drop elsewhere cancels (also when the dragged pane has left the screen with the page). The panes show each page's agents in their order, so the screen always matches the saved order (no separate pane arrangement since #134). **Move Left / Move Right** (agent menu; commands `agent.moveLeft` / `agent.moveRight`, Ctrl+Alt+Shift+Left/Right on the focused agent) cross pages at the edges. Moving stops nothing: running terminals stay, and the moved agent keeps focus (so the view goes to its page). Names don't change. Not for the Hive Assistant (one agent). |
| Discard (worktree agent) | Stops it, deletes the worktree and branch, removes the agent. |
| Remove All (#291) | Every agent of the project, from the project header's **Remove All (n)** ("Remove Agent" with one; in ⋯ under 640 px of header), the Session menu and the palette ("Remove All Agents (n)…", `session.removeAll`). User-only: not in the Agent API or the Assistant's tools. One question in the danger style lists every agent (running ones flagged as Start New's are) and, for a worktree agent, whether its worktree is *merged and clean* or *always kept* and why; running agents are stopped first; sessions stay. **Worktrees and branches are kept by default.** A tick box, offered only when some worktree qualifies and naming them, deletes those whose branch is fully merged into the repository's **main branch** (the local branch origin/HEAD names, else `main`, else `master`: `primaryBranch()`; never the project folder's checked-out branch, which can be any feature branch; a squash merge counts) with no uncommitted changes, as git lists them (`worktreeCheck()` in `main/worktrees.ts`, shared with #289). The deletion is guarded (`removeCheckedWorktree()`): main checks again just before, refuses (touching nothing) if the main branch isn't the one the question named, or the worktree's branch or the main branch no longer points at the commit checked (mergedness is checked against the main branch's commit, not its name), removes the worktree **without** `--force` (so git keeps one with files written since the check) and deletes the branch in one git ref transaction (`update-ref --stdin`) that verifies the main branch is still at the commit it was found merged into and deletes the branch only from the commit checked; if either moved in the last moment the branch is kept and the notice says so; it never deletes a worktree another agent of the project still works in, and never uses Discard's forced removal. Unmerged or dirty work is never deleted from a batch. The agents' open cards are asked about once for all of them (move back or leave, as for one agent). One agent at a time, every tab showing the removing spinner until its agent goes; a failure leaves that agent and the rest still go, and one notice names the agents that couldn't be removed and why, with the worktrees deleted and kept (also after a ticked removal that succeeded). |
| Unused worktrees (#353) | The git worktrees of a project that no agent of it works in (`unusedWorktrees.ts`): kept when their agent was removed with "Keep worktree and branch" (Remove Agent, Remove All without its box) or made outside Hive. Only folders git lists as the project's worktrees, that exist, aren't the project folder, don't contain it or the workspace and aren't another project (or the Assistant's home). Each is checked with #291's `worktreeCheck` (merged into the main branch and clean; `ahead` and `dirty` counted; anything git can't say is not removable, #346), with its last commit and the commit its folder has checked out. They live in the **Changes** tab (#400; until then the Overview had them). Its picker (#476, `ChangesPicker`) leads with them: **Unused worktrees (n)** (`changesRoot` `unused`; "(looking…)" until the first list) and each one on a branch (`unused:<folder>`, "hive/b4 · was B4 · holds work": `unusedPickerLabel`), then **Worktrees in use** (each agent's), then **Project folder**, last (agents working in the folder itself, reviewers, solo users). With nothing chosen, the tab opens on Unused worktrees when the project has worktrees (its agents' or unused ones), else on the project folder, with no picker; when that isn't known yet (no agent's worktree and no list yet), on Unused worktrees, looking, until a list says: a first listing that fails is never taken for "none" (the page stays, with the error and Retry, the picker saying "couldn't list"), and only a list that comes back empty means the project folder. Each page says what it shows in one sentence, in one place: at the top of its right side (`CHANGES_ABOUT`: unused worktrees and what can be done with them, in the Unused worktrees header, `UnusedHeading`, also while loading and when empty; one unused worktree; an agent's worktree since it left its base; the project folder, each above its diff). The left panel holds the picker and the files only. **Who made each** (`origin`, `worktreeOrigin`): the Hive agent whose session last ran in it (sessions.json keeps each session's folder and agent name: "was B4"), else Hive when it is in Hive's worktrees folder for the project ("made by Hive"), else "not made by Hive" (an agent's own `git worktree add`, a CLI subagent's isolation, the user's), shown in the picker and as a badge on each row. **Loading**: listing reads git for each worktree, which takes a while. Each project's last list is kept in the window, with when it was read (`useUnusedWorktrees`, a `RecentCache` of the 20 projects shown last, emptied when the window's workspace changes), and shown at once on coming back, with "Checking them again…" while git is asked again; with no list yet the page shows a spinner and "Looking for unused worktrees…"; the header's **Refresh** looks again (its icon turns while it does). A listing that fails says so: with no list, why, with Retry; over a list from before, that list stays with "Could not refresh the unused worktrees; last updated …" and Retry (`StaleNote`). With git missing, the page is the tab's git-missing page. The list (`UnusedWorktreesSection`, reloaded when the agents' worktrees change or `bumpUnused` after a merge; an empty message at 0) shows each row with its branch (or detached), its state ("Merged into main · clean", "3 commits not on main · 2 changed files", or git's reason), who made it, last commit, and size (from Storage's measurement, cancellable). **Remove** (merged and clean) and **Remove all merged (n)…** (one confirm listing them) call `worktrees:removeUnused`, which reserves the folder (`reserveForRemoval`, so `addAgent` and template loads refuse it under the project file's lock), checks under that lock that no agent works in it, that git still lists it and that it is safe to touch, then `removeCheckedWorktree` with the main branch shown (never `--force`; the branch deleted in the guarded ref transaction). One that holds work has **Give to an agent…** (Add Agent with Existing worktree and that folder chosen, `addAgentPreset`) and **Remove anyway…** (offered only when git could count what it holds). Remove anyway asks main for a **preview** first (`worktrees:removalPreview`): a snapshot of what the removal would delete, taken now (its canonical folder, its branch or detached HEAD, the commit checked out, the main branch and its commit, read strictly (`primaryBranchStrict`: a branch listing or origin/HEAD git couldn't read is a failure, never "no main branch"), the commits only it holds and its uncommitted files, and a fingerprint of everything a forced removal discards: every entry in the folder, ignored ones included, by path, kind and whole content (streamed; a link by its target, never followed), and its git index as `git ls-files --stage` lists it (staged content); a folder of more than 300,000 entries or 4 GB isn't fingerprinted), kept in main under a token that is spent by the first removal that presents it (whatever comes of it) and refused once older than 15 minutes (#373: checked when it is presented, not only when another preview prunes old ones; "look again"); a refusal a new look settles (expired, spent, or the worktree changed: `UnusedWorktreeRemoval.lookAgain`) closes the question, never offering Try Again with the spent token, and the window takes a new preview and asks again, saying why nothing was removed and listing what it holds now, its own token and explicit confirmation (#377); other refusals (an agent works in it now) are said in a notice; anything git or the folder can't say means no preview (nothing to confirm). The danger confirm lists what that preview found goes ("1 commit on hive/x not on main", "1 commit on no branch (only its detached HEAD has it)", "2 uncommitted files in …", the ignored files). Confirmed, the removal presents the token and main takes the snapshot again: if anything differs (a file renamed or rewritten, an ignored file changed, another branch checked out at the same commit, the main branch moved), nothing is removed and the dialog says what changed; otherwise the folder goes (forced) and the branch the preview named, only from the commit it showed (`update-ref -d`). A detached worktree's work is the commits its HEAD holds that no branch, tag or remote branch has (`rev-list HEAD --not --branches --tags --remotes`), which count as work (the Changes notice, no plain Remove). A removed worktree leaves `setupPending` and Storage's cache. The **Changes** tab shows a notice only while an unused worktree holds work ("2 unused worktrees have work that isn't on main — Review"). **Storage** measures them (`ProjectStorage.unusedWorktrees`, in the total) and shows one line, Unused worktrees (n), with Review. Hints, one notification each: after Remove Agent keeping its worktree ("Merged's worktree is kept", **Review unused worktrees**), after Remove All leaving any (one notice for all), and after deleting a template whose worktree agents' worktrees (named as Hive names them: `hive/<slug>` in `<worktrees>/<project>/<slug>`, or numbered) are unused (`templates:delete` returns them per project; "3 unused worktrees in hive — Review"); the delete itself touches no worktree. Review opens the project's Overview at the section (`project.unusedWorktrees`, `unusedJump`). The Agent API's `GET /v1/projects/{name}` (`hive_project_status`) adds `unusedWorktrees: { count, merged }` when there are any, so the Assistant can mention them; there is no route to remove them (user-only). Without git nothing is listed (the reason instead) and nothing removed. **Open** (a row's, on a branch) or picking one in the folder picker shows it as an agent's worktree is shown: its changes since the branch it would merge into (`git:status`/`git:diff` with that base; a diff's **Open file** opens that folder's copy, `changes:openFile`, which takes only a folder the Changes tab may read and a file inside it by real path, so an unused worktree outside the workspace opens too, #495), and, when it holds work, **Merge** (the Merge dialog, `mergeFor` naming the worktree by folder and branch): `worktrees:unusedBranchStatus` and `worktrees:mergeUnused` take only a folder the project's unused-worktree listing would list (on a branch), and merge it as an agent's merge goes, fenced from the start as a removal is (`reserveForRemoval`: an Add Agent or template load refuses it until the merge is over, and under the project file's lock no agent may have it already, so the merge never stages or commits an agent's files) (`mergeInProject`: one at a time per project folder under its `hive-merge` lock, holding the merge slot of the branch it merges into, #350); its "Remove the worktree and branch afterwards" goes through `removeUnusedWorktree`'s checked removal (merged and clean now, else kept: `MergeResult.cleanupKept`, said in a notice). Changes reads an unused worktree's git status and diffs through `assertChangesRoot` (a project folder, an agent's worktree, or one of the project's unused worktrees as last listed, `noteUnusedWorktrees`; replaced on each listing): only those git reads, never the Files tab's file operations (`assertRoot`). The **Overview** shows one line (`UnusedWorktreesLine`): "7 unused worktrees (7 merged) — Review in Changes" (`unusedSummary`), opening Changes at the list (`showUnusedWorktrees`, also the `project.unusedWorktrees` command, the notices' Review and Storage's "Review in Changes"). Changes' project folder keeps its notice when one holds work (`unusedWorkNotice`), its Review opening the list. |
| Hand Over to… (agent menu, or the footer's transcript size) | Hands the agent's work to another agent of the project, of any provider (conversations can't move between providers), or to **itself, in a new conversation**: after the handover, Hive stops it (waiting up to 15 s), starts a new conversation and gives it the handover, which makes a long transcript short again (the old conversation stays in the Sessions tab). Optionally the source (running and idle) is first asked to write a handover with `hive_create_handover`; Hive waits until a handover for the project newer than the ones before exists (among the 10 newest, as another agent may write one after it), written in the source's own session (its header's **Session**; another agent's handover meanwhile doesn't count) (up to 15 minutes; if the agent finishes, asks something or stops without writing one, Hive says so and nothing starts). The target then starts a new session (waiting up to 5 minutes for it to be ready, e.g. while the user answers a trust question), or, if it is running and idle, gets the message in its session: read that handover, named by its path, with `hive_read_shared_note`, and continue (without a new handover: the latest one, named the same way; refused when there is none). **Handover header**: Hive writes it, not the agent: `# <title>`, then `- **Project:**`, `- **Author:** <agent> (<CLI>)` (or `Assistant`), `- **Session:** <id>` and `- **Date:** <machine time> (<UTC>)`. Each agent's hive MCP server gets its agent's id (`HIVE_AGENT_ID`) and passes it with `hive_create_handover`; Hive looks up the agent's name, CLI and current session. Handovers written another way (the Notes view, a script through the Agent API) have no Author or Session; older handovers are left as they are. The target's session record gets `handedOverFrom`; the Sessions tab shows a **handed over** badge linking to the source session. Needs Hive's tools in sessions; busy agents can't be chosen. Also `POST /v1/projects/{name}/handover`. |

### 6.3 Status via HTTP hooks

`launch/settings.json` registers Claude Code **HTTP hooks** pointing at Hive's local hook server (`127.0.0.1`, random port per app run). The token is sent as `Authorization: Bearer $HIVE_HOOK_TOKEN` using `allowedEnvVars`. `SessionStart` only supports command hooks (and `mcp_tool`; checked against the hooks reference with 2.1.292), so it is a command that pipes the hook JSON to the same server with `curl.exe`.

**Hook tokens** (#345): **one per launch** (`hookTokens.ts`), made when the launch starts and ended with it (its exit, or a start given up), never one for the whole Hive run. The hook server takes a call only with the token of the launch its `?run=` names (`timingSafeEqual`), so a token can't speak for another session or outlive its own; a call naming no launch is refused (hooks of an ended launch were already ignored). HTTP hooks read it from the session's environment. **No file Hive generates in a project, and no argument it passes, holds a secret.** Each launch has a **private folder** in Hive's user data, `launches/<run id>/` (`launchPrivateDir()`), removed when the launch ends and all of them when Hive starts (earlier runs' tokens are dead). It holds the hook auth file (`hook-auth.txt`) and whatever of the launch may carry a secret: Claude Code's `settings.json` (with a user's `--settings` and its `env`, #330) and `mcp.json` (the workspace's MCP servers' own `env`, headers and arguments). The project's launch folder keeps only what holds none: the skills plugin, `sync.json`, the Assistant's `instructions.md`, `README.txt`. Hook commands (Claude Code's SessionStart and status line, every Codex hook) read the header with `curl -H "@<file>"` from the auth file. Codex gets MCP servers as `-c` arguments and already leaves out one with a literal secret (it would show in process listings), with a warning; the hive MCP server's definition names the agent's token file, not the token. `tests/hookTokens.test.ts` scans what both providers write and pass, with a synthetic token, MCP env and header secrets and a `--settings` env. The command is `curl.exe -s -m 5 -X POST -H "@<auth file>" -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@-" "<url>"` for both CLIs (`hookForwardCommand()`): `curl.exe` unquoted and `"@-"` quoted, forward slashes, which works in Git Bash, PowerShell (Claude Code's fallback without Git Bash, and Codex's shell) and cmd alike (Windows 11, curl 8.21 and Git's 8.7.1; before, Claude Code's quoted curl path didn't run under PowerShell). Claude Code's `allowedHttpHookUrls`/`httpHookAllowedEnvVars` in a user's `--settings` get Hive's URL and variable (#330). 

| Hook | Hive status / action |
|---|---|
| `SessionStart` | **ready** |
| `PreToolUse` (every tool, since #648) | A call starts (the turn ledger, below: a tool after its turn's Stop carries the turn on). Edit, Write, MultiEdit and NotebookEdit get the file lock check (below), answered before the tool runs; any other tool is answered at once |
| `UserPromptSubmit`, `PostToolUse` | **working** (not a `PostToolUse` of a turn that is over: the turn ledger) |
| `Notification` (permission prompt) | **waiting** — notification if window hidden/unfocused |
| `Notification` (`idle_prompt`, "Claude is waiting for your input") | Claude Code sends it once, a minute after a turn ends, only while no turn runs and no dialog is open (2.1.296): an agent waiting on a safeguard prompt that was answered is idle after the stop (*Safeguard stops*, below); an agent Hive still shows **working** is **ready** again (**background** with background tasks counted), its file locks released, and the log says so with the launch's last eight hooks (kind, time, the status each left), never what was said (#612: HB3 stayed "Working…" after its last turn, so prompts to it were refused; its Stop was delivered, and why Hive didn't act on it is still unknown). Any other status stands, and so does Hive's own Compact. Claude Code's compaction while idle (before the prompt cache expires: `PreCompact` with trigger `auto`, `SessionStart` with source `compact`, `PostCompact`) and its recap (an `away_summary` in the transcript, with no hook Hive takes) leave an idle agent idle (`PreCompact`/`PostCompact` below) |
| `Stop` | **finished** — chime if enabled; notification if hidden/unfocused. **background** instead when the agent has background tasks running that will start it again (below) |
| `StopFailure` | The turn ended on an API error instead of Stop. `error: "authentication_failed"` (`last_assistant_message` "Login expired · Please run /login", 2.1.291) is a refused sign-in: **signin** (below). Any other error (a rate limit, the server) ends the turn as `Stop` does, with the CLI's message |
| `PreCompact` | **working** ("Compacting the conversation…" when it was idle, e.g. `/compact` typed in the CLI); toast for automatic compaction |
| `PostCompact` | back to **ready** after a compaction that started idle (Hive's Compact or `/compact`); one mid-turn keeps working, and so does a turn begun since (a prompt after the compaction began ends it). Without the hook, Hive's Compact ends when the transcript shows a new compaction, the CLI says it refused or failed it, or if it hasn't begun 20 seconds after Hive submitted its `/compact` (typing a long focus doesn't count) or runs past 10 minutes, with the same rule. While Hive types the command, the agent is reserved: a second Compact or a prompt is refused, and if the agent stops meanwhile the rest isn't typed |
| `SessionEnd` | **stopped** |

Hooks have a short timeout; if Hive is unreachable, Claude Code continues normally. The same question twice, or a second `Stop`, notifies once. The rules are in `main/hookStatus.ts` (pure, unit-tested for both providers); `SessionManager.handleHookNow` carries out what they say.

**Another conversation in the same CLI** (Claude Code `/clear` or `/resume`, Codex `/new`): its first prompt's hook carries a different session id for the same launch. Hive then backs up the old conversation, records the new one (as the agent's session to resume) and follows it — usage, backups, the context meter. Only a prompt switches: helpers a CLI runs for itself (subagents) can report other ids.

Hooks are normalised by each adapter into one set of events (start, prompt, tool start/end, needs input, stop, interrupt, compact start/end, end), so the status rules above are shared by every provider.

**The turn ledger** (#648, `main/turnLedger.ts`, pure and unit-tested): status follows the CLI's **turns by their own ids**, so a late or missing hook can't freeze it. Each adapter keeps a hook's ids through normalisation (`NormalizedHook.ids`): the turn (Claude Code's `prompt_id`, Codex's `turn_id`; Copilot's hooks name none, 1.0.95), the tool call (`tool_use_id`), the sub-agent that sent it (Claude Code's `agent_id`) and whether a Stop follows a continuation (`stop_hook_active`). Every hook goes through it before the status rules. **Only a start opens or reopens a turn**: a prompt opens one, and after its Stop a turn reopens only on a start of it (a tool call it never started, or Hive's own continuation of its Stop); end news (a tool end, an interrupt, the CLI idle, a refused sign-in, a Stop) never reopens one. Every hook that names a turn is that turn's news: one naming a turn that is neither the open nor the latest one (a prompt, a question, a tool, a Stop, an interrupt, the CLI idle, a refused sign-in) is stale: logged, with no effect on status, and never makes that turn active over a newer one. **An id Hive doesn't remember is stale, never new** (the ledger keeps the latest 10,000 ids, only to say in the log whether one was an earlier turn's): only a prompt opens a turn under an id never seen, since the CLI awaits a prompt's hook before the turn. This holds for every hook that names a turn, launch-wide ones too: a delayed compaction or session end of an earlier turn is stale. Two starts the CLI makes by itself come under an id of their own with no prompt hook (Claude Code 2.1.296, verified in a test home): **a compaction** (`/compact` while idle, or its own), whose start, while no turn runs, opens that compaction, not a turn (Darren, option B), and only its own news (its end, the session's restart and end) follows under that id until a turn begins; it begins and ends once, so its start again (while it runs or after its end) and its news once it is over change nothing, but for the session's end; and **a background task's wake**, which sends its prompt hook (the task's notification) and so opens like any prompt. Should that prompt hook be lost, a tool call the launch never saw opens the wake's turn, but only while the CLI's transcript says a task of it just ended, never merely because one runs (Darren, 12 Oct). Past the bound on the ids kept, neither opens anything. Hooks naming no turn are launch-wide (SessionStart, a compaction's) or from a CLI whose hooks name none, and are the latest turn's. A turn begins when its first hook reaches Hive, not when Hive gets round to it in the launch's queue (the CLI's records are compared with that).
- A **prompt** begins a turn, except one in the turn that runs (the same id: a message the CLI takes mid-turn). One Hive put into the running turn itself (`expectInjected`, for Messaging 5's socket delivery, which fires `UserPromptSubmit` with the running turn's `prompt_id`) is no prompt at all.
- A **tool end of a turn that is over** is old news and changes nothing: a `PostToolUse` that reaches Hive after its turn's `Stop` no longer makes a finished agent working (one way #642's Codex reviewer could have stayed working for 37 minutes). So is a tool end naming a turn never seen (its start would have begun it). Without turn ids the same holds for a tool end after the turn's end with no tool start since. An earlier turn's `Stop` changes nothing either.
- A **tool start of the latest turn after its Stop**, for a call the turn never started, carries that turn on (another Stop hook, the user's, made the CLI go on): working again, decided from the ids alone, so a hook that reaches Hive before the CLI has redrawn changes nothing (Copilot's hooks name no calls: any tool start after its Stop carries it on). A start of a call the turn started before its Stop is no start of it. A delayed start that wasn't one leaves the agent working until the silence below shows its end. With no ids and no turn known yet, a tool start alone changes nothing. Claude Code's `PreToolUse` is registered for every tool for this. A sub-agent's tools are its parent's turn's while it runs, and don't carry on one that is over; a sub-agent's Stop ends nothing.
- A **Stop ends its turn only when Hive answered it with nothing**: continuations (a block, or context the CLI acts on: `adapter.stopContinues()`) fire no prompt, so a Stop Hive carried on leaves the turn running (Claude Code 2.1.296 and Codex 0.162.1, verified in #645's spike). The hook server asks `sessions.hookReply()` for a Stop's reply (`stopReplier`, for Messaging 4's drain; none yet) and passes it on with the hook.
- An interrupt, a refused sign-in or the CLI saying it is idle at its prompt (`idle_prompt`) ends the running turn.

**Reconciliation, and unknown ≠ idle.** When a Stop never comes, Hive looks for the turn's end in what the CLI keeps, never on a timer:
- **The CLI's own record**: Codex's `task_complete` for that turn id in its rollout (`LiveDetails.turnEnded`, written after its Stop hooks are answered), which ends only the running turn of that id; Claude Code's `idle_prompt` notification (above). Copilot's records don't name turns as its hooks do, so Hive counts when its Stops were answered (its `agentStop` `hook.end`, `LiveDetails.stopsAnswered`, this launch's): more answered since the running turn's first hook reached Hive than Stops of that turn Hive got (each one it carried the turn on with counts) means a Stop never reached Hive, and the turn is over (`unheardStop`). One answered before the turn began is an earlier turn's, also when read in the same lines as the new prompt's hook; a final Stop missed after Hive's continuations still counts. Either ends the turn as its Stop would: finished, told, locks released, and the log says so with the hooks before.
- **Silence** (Settings has no knob: 5 minutes with nothing heard, no hook and no transcript growth, tool calls open or not, since their ends can be lost too; `HIVE_TEST_SILENT_MS` in unpackaged test builds) makes Hive look at the CLI's record again and read its screen with a **version-tested check** (`adapter.turnScreen(version)`: idle, working, or not sure), checked against screens recorded from the real CLIs, alone and in Hive's terminals. **Idle**: Claude Code 2.1.296's status line above its input (marked "❯", or ">" as it draws it in Hive) says the last turn is done ("✻ Worked for 12s · done 16:52") with nothing below offering to interrupt; Codex 0.162.1's input is empty, showing its placeholder ("Ask Codex to do anything"), with nothing queued. **Working**: Claude Code's spinner on that line ("✢ Embellishing…"; it shows no "esc to interrupt" in Hive's terminals), Codex's "• Working (5s • esc to interrupt)". Idle on screen: the turn is over, as its Stop would end it. Working on screen (a long reply or think, a long tool): the CLI is heard, so it isn't silent. Older versions, and Copilot, aren't read.
- Failing both, the agent's state is **unknown** (with a tool call open, which may be a long test run, only after 12 times as long a silence): still **working** (so nothing is typed into it and it isn't given work), its pane saying **"Silent 23 min · state unknown"** with **Mark idle**, the log saying what Hive saw. Anything heard of it (a hook, its transcript growing) makes it running again; **Mark idle** (`session:markIdle`, the user only) ends the turn: **ready** (or **background**), its locks released, not told as finished, and what Hive thought ran with it (its open tool calls, a compaction, a person asked) is over too. The Agent API's `statusMessage` says it ("Silent 23 min · state unknown: … the user can mark it idle"). A card loop's stall line names the reviewer's real state ("HR3 is watching #6 but hasn't started reviewing it (it shows working, but has been silent for 23 min: state unknown)"; "(it is working on something else)" when it works), not just "watching" (#642).

**Status as fields** (`LiveSessionState.runtime`, `.turn` and `.backgroundTasks`, gathered by `shared/runtime.ts` `agentRuntime()`): separate facts, each stored where Hive learns it, never read back from the status word. **Lifecycle** (starting, running once the CLI reports anything or shows its prompt, needs sign-in from a refused sign-in until a request of its own works, stopped). **Turn** (running, over, unknown, none; with the CLI's id: the ledger). **Maintenance**: compacting from `PreCompact`, mid-turn too, or Hive's Compact, until `PostCompact`, the turn's end, a prompt starting a new turn or Hive's Compact ending. **Attention**: waiting when the status rules make the agent wait for a person, until they move it on; a question while one waits beside its work. **Composer**: held while Hive types into its input, confirms a wake or waits for a typed line to be taken (or one wasn't), else free. **Background tasks**. The labels (the pane, the dot's tooltip, `statusText`) are projected from these facts (`runtimeLabel()`: stopped, asking, starting, needs sign-in, compacting, silent with its state unknown, working); when no turn runs, Hive's policy words apply: watching, finished or ready, background tasks. The status word stays as the coarse gate today's producers use (Messaging 3 moves them onto deliveries). `GET /v1/projects/{name}/agents/{agent}/activity?detail=true` (`hive_agent_activity` with detail) gives them as `runtime`; the short form's `statusMessage` says when the state is unknown.

**Hook journal** (`logs/hooks.log`, beside `hive.log`, kept to 5 MB and one older copy): each hook a CLI sent, with its launch (`run=`), its receive order (`seq=`), event, turn and call ids, sub-agent, `continued`, the status before and after, and what the ledger made of it (stale and why, carried on by Hive), so the next stuck status can be traced (#642's couldn't). Ids and statuses only, never the user's text; reconciliations and Mark idle are journaled too.

**Background tasks.** An agent can leave work running when its turn ends: a test run, a server, a watch. Each adapter reads them from the transcript as it grows (`backgroundTasks()`), and Hive counts the ones started since the agent launched (`backgroundTasks` in its live state; shown as "· 2 background tasks running").
- **Claude Code**: a background Bash call's result has `backgroundTaskId` (also a command moved to the background after running too long), a Monitor's has `taskId` and an expiry. A task ends with a `<task-notification>` naming it (any status but a Monitor's events) or a `TaskStop`. Claude Code is told when a task ends and starts a new turn by itself, so the provider has the `backgroundWakes` capability: a turn that ends with tasks open leaves the agent **background** ("Waiting on 2 background tasks"), not finished. When the last one ends, the agent is **working** again (the notification's turn), then finished at its Stop. No "finished" notification or chime until then.
- **Codex**: a command still running when `exec_command` returns gives a `session_id` instead of an `exit_code`; Codex records its end as an `item_completed` CommandExecution with that `process_id`. In code mode (0.160) the command runs inside an `exec` script that prints what the model chooses, and Codex records no start (only each command's end, and no record of which `exec_command` calls a script ran), so a start needs evidence: an id the output shows (the result's whole-number `session_id`, or a script that reads `session_id`, printed only numbers, such as `text(r.session_id)`, and had none of its commands end within it), ended by its `process_id`; or else a script that runs its one `exec_command` once (a real call, not one in a string or a comment, as a statement of its own at the top level, with no loop, condition, function or `try` in the script) and completed with none of its commands ended, since Codex writes the end of every command that ends within a script before the script's output (counted under a stand-in id, ended by the CommandExecution of the same command text). Anything less clear (a loop that printed no ids, a condition, a failed or aborted script, an end that could be the script's own) counts nothing: a command missed rather than one counted that isn't running (`providers/codex/background.ts`, which keeps what it needs between reads of the rollout). Codex isn't told when one ends (checked with 0.159), so its agents stay **finished** and the count is only shown.
- **Time limit.** Hive can't tell a test run from something that never ends (a dev server), so a task stops counting after **Settings → Agents & Worktrees → Count background tasks for up to** (60 minutes; 10–480), and a Monitor at its own expiry if sooner. Hive logs it, and an agent left background then shows finished ("Stopped counting its background tasks after 60 minutes"), with the notification.
- A **background** agent counts as busy: `hive_wait_for_agents` waits through it (unless `ignoreBackground`), `hive_prompt_agent`, Hand Over, Compact and Merge refuse it, *Confirm before quitting* asks (its tasks would stop), and *Quit when agents finish* waits for it. The sidebar dot is a slow, faint pulse.
- **Needs sign-in** (`signin`, #309) is the status of an agent whose CLI refused its turn because the sign-in has expired or was refused. Each adapter says so (no provider ids elsewhere): Claude Code by `StopFailure` with `authentication_failed`; Codex, which sends no hook then, by its rollout: a `task_complete` whose `error.codex_error_info` is `unauthorized` or carries HTTP 401 (0.160.0, an invalid API key: `http_connection_failed: { http_status_code: 401 }`), else only Codex's own sign-in messages (a 401 status line, a refresh token that expired, was used or was revoked) — `LiveDetails.signIn`, applied in order with the launch's hooks (not when a hook came while it was being read: the next prompt is newer than the refusal, #480). Before this, both CLIs' agents stayed **working** for ever. The turn ends (locks released, not told as finished) and the agent keeps the CLI's message (`LiveSessionState.signIn`, shown in its tooltips; the status line says **Needs sign-in**). It needs the user (the inbox, the taskbar count, `hive_wait_for_agents` stops for it, `hive_prompt_agent` refuses it; its `statusMessage` there says why). One notice per CLI and expiry, a few seconds after the first agent so agents refused together are counted together ("3 agents need you to sign in to Claude Code", with the descriptor's `signInHelp`); a later agent in the same expiry is shown, not told. Hive checks the CLI's sign-in at once (Agent Setup shows it signed out) and every minute while agents wait. It is **signed in again** when a request of an agent of that CLI works (a tool call, or a turn that ends well: Claude Code carries the turn on after `/login` in its terminal; not a prompt merely sent, an interrupt, or a turn that failed on something else, such as a rate limit — Claude Code's other `StopFailure` errors are `stop` events marked `failed`), when its check goes from signed out to signed in, or when Agent Setup's Sign in task ends well (`providerService.onSignedIn`). Until then the expiry lasts: an agent it stopped keeps `signIn` through a retry that doesn't get through (its own prompt or Resume's shows **working** meanwhile; interrupted, or failed on something else, it is **signin** again, not told as finished), and the minute's recheck ends the expiry quietly only once no agent it stopped is running. Then the agents still stopped are **ready** with "Stopped while signed out" and a toast; until a request of their own works they keep `signIn`, and **Resume All Agents (n)** counts them (`stalledOnSignIn()` in `shared/resumeAll.ts`): it gives each a short "carry on" (`session:carryOn`; a control delivery for that launch, given now, #649), never one that has carried on by itself, and never unasked. Codex's behaviour after signing in again mid-session wasn't observed.
- **Watching** is the status of an agent whose turn has ended with a card watch running (§6.6, *Waiting on cards*): its `ready`/`finished` shows as `watching` (`SessionManager.emitState`), and when the watch ends it is `finished` again. It counts as busy like `background`: *Confirm before quitting* asks, for quitting and for closing a window or workspace or switching it (the dialog says what it waits for: stopping pauses its card loop until it is resumed, the watch kept); the Assistant's stop asks the user first; Merge is refused until it has finished or its watch is cancelled (merging commits unfinished work); `hive_prompt_agent` and Start Task refuse it. *Quit when agents finish* waits for it only while a card it watches is in Doing or Review with another agent (`watchKeepsQuitWaiting`, the same rule for the dialog's offer and the wait); once the watches have seen a watched card move or change hands (`onWatchedCardsMoved`), a pending quit checks again, so it goes ahead when no such card is left even though no wake comes. But `hive_wait_for_agents` doesn't wait for it (it reports `watching`, with the watch's label as `statusMessage`), and Compact is allowed from the header, the agent menu and the dialog (the watch is kept; a wake waits until the compaction has ended). No "finished" notification or chime while it watches. The sidebar dot is a hollow ring in the info colour.
- Hive looks at a running transcript every 5 seconds and reads it again when its size or modified time changes: Windows can report an old modified time for a file Codex keeps appending to.

**Safeguard stops** (#606, Darren's decisions of 11 and 12 Oct). A CLI's safety classifier can stop an agent's response (Opus 5.5's safeguards stopped one of HB3's on 11 Oct), ask what to do about it, or switch the agent to another model by itself (Claude Code's fallback when `/config` says to switch models when a message is flagged). Hive shows when, on which model, and the **names** of the agent's last few tool calls before it (5), never the stopped content (the CLI withholds it anyway) nor a call's input.
- **What it is told by**, as named defaults in one place (`SAFEGUARD_DEFAULTS` in `shared/safeguards.ts`: data, not code), the note and the question Advanced settings (§9.3, #685: `claude-code.safeguard.note`, `.prompt`) that the detection reads through `advanced`, so an override changes them; the record subtypes and Codex's codes join them once a real session of one is recorded (#697): **Claude Code**'s transcript (`claudeSafeguards()`, its 2.1.296 records): a stop it retries once is its system note ("Opus 5.5's safeguards stopped the response above · continuing once with that noted", subtype `informational`, matched by the `claudeNote` pattern) and, as the next entry in its chain, the notice it adds for the model (a user entry with `refusedMessageId`, the stopped response); a flagged request another model answered, or none did, is its record of it (`claudeRecords`: subtypes `model_refusal_fallback`, with `originalModel`, `fallbackModel` and `scope`, and `model_refusal_no_fallback`), which for the session (`scope: session`) is also a switch the safeguard made; a fallback for another reason (its `trigger` not `refusal`) isn't one. Its own messages (`/feedback`'s `local_command`) and side chains don't count. A model change in its assistant messages after a stop is a switch, except the one response another model answered alone (`scope: local`). **Codex** (`codexSafeguards()`, from its 0.160 binary: no rollout of one seen yet): a `model_reroute` event whose reason is in `codexReroute` (`high_risk_cyber_activity`) is a stop and a switch from `from_model` to `to_model`; an `error` or `task_complete` with a `codex_error_info` in `codexStop` (`cyber_policy`, `bio_policy`) a stop.
- **One stop, counted once, by its identity, never by time** (`SafeguardEvent.id`, kept in the detector's memo: `newStop()`): Claude Code's note and its notice are one stop (adjacent, or the notice's `parentUuid` the note's `uuid`); a notice alone is a stop by its `refusedMessageId`, a record by its `requestId`; Codex's a stop per turn (its `turn_id`, or the count of turns started) and kind. A record read again is no other stop, and two stops seconds apart are two. **Copilot**'s session events have no equivalent: **not detected** (`safeguardHelp: null` in its descriptor), and Hive says so where it would show one.
- **The agent's note** (`LiveSessionState.safeguard`, `noteSafeguard()`): the last stop's time, model and tool names, the stops this launch, the switch (from, to, since) until the model changes back, and whether it waits on the CLI's prompt about it or is idle after one. Each stop a detector or the screen check gives it counts. Its pane shows a shield beside the status (amber while it waits, is idle after one, or runs on another model, with that model's name), its tooltip saying what happened and the provider's pointers (Claude Code: `/feedback` for a false positive, `/model` to switch back, `/config` for the switch setting); the agent's tab tooltip says it too.
- **A switch** is told: Hive's notice ("HB3 now runs on Opus 4.8"), a comment by Hive on the agent's Doing cards (`commentOnDoingCards()`), and the attention inbox (**After a safeguard stop**) until the agent's pane has been on screen (`safeguard.unseen`, cleared by `markSeen`).
- **The prompt about a stop.** Claude Code asks whether to switch models (a question it puts through its permission check, header "Model switch": "…'s safeguards flagged this message. … Switch to Opus 4.8 and keep going whenever this happens?", choices **Switch automatically** and **Stay on** its model) with a `Notification` (`permission_prompt`) naming no tool ("Claude needs your permission"), after its turn's `Stop` (so the turn ledger finds it stale), and the stop that asked HB3 left no record in its transcript. So a blocking ask naming no tool, from a CLI whose adapter tells a safeguard prompt by its screen (`safeguardAsk()`: the `claudePrompt` pattern, the open dialog's two numbered choices, in the last 24 lines it wrote; never the words "safeguards" or "safety classifier" in task text or an earlier stop's note, nor an answered prompt, which shows only its answer), is checked against the screen when it arrives and as the screen draws, for 10 seconds; once it is one, the agent **waits** (even after its turn's Stop) and the status and notification say why, with the last tool calls ("A safety classifier stopped a response: Claude Code asks what to do (its last tool calls: Bash, Edit)"). The prompt is a stop (once, however often it is told while it waits), kept on the session's record (`SessionRecord.screenStops`) for Performance with its **turn** (`SafeguardEvent.turn`: the uuid of the prompt that started it, in the transcript's order; a prompt is any user entry with text, markup and all, or images alone, that isn't Claude Code's own: meta entries, a compaction's summary, tool results). Claude Code's record of a stop it asked about (`model_refusal_fallback` after **Switch automatically**: `SafeguardEvent.asked`) carries its turn the same way, so in a turn one prompt and one record are the same stop, whichever Hive reads first, however late (after the next prompt) and across a restart: the agent's note counts it once (`seenInTurn()`), and so does Performance, which matches them from what is kept (`SessionUsage.safeguardsAsked` per turn, `withScreenStops()`). A second prompt in a turn, a record in a turn with no prompt, and a prompt with no turn known are each a stop of their own. The CLI sends no hook for the answer, so HB3 showed "Claude needs your permission" for half an hour: the wait ends (`safeguardAnswered()` in `hookStatus.ts`) when its `idle_prompt` comes, or the prompt is gone from its screen, once the user has typed in its terminal since it asked (or the prompt is no longer on screen at all). It is then **ready** with "Idle after a safeguard stop: give it a nudge" (or **background** on its tasks) until it is given something to do.
- **The Assistant** sees it in `hive_agent_activity`: `safeguard` with when, the stops, the model, the tool names before it, the model switched to and from and since when, and whether it waits on the prompt or is idle after one; `"not detected"` for a CLI Hive can't tell. **Performance** counts the stops (below).

**File locks.** Hooks are matched to sessions by `session_id`, so every agent reports its own status. For file edits, `PreToolUse` asks Hive first: the first agent to edit a file claims it (by its real path, so a junction or link to the same file is the same file; the file is named to agents, in the agent's locked files and in Agent API activity relative to that agent's folder, by real paths when the folder and the file are written two ways: `placeRelative`, #494); while the claim holds, another agent's edit of the same file gets, depending on the lock mode, `permissionDecision: "deny"` with a reason naming the holder (**Block**, default), `"ask"` (**Ask me**: Claude Code's permission prompt), or `additionalContext` warning it (**Warn**); **Off** skips the check. For CLIs whose hooks can't hand an edit to their own approval prompt (Codex rejects `"ask"` and then runs the tool; descriptor capability `lockAsk`), **Ask me** denies the edit, telling the agent to stop and wait, and Hive shows a notification with **Allow**; Allow lets that agent edit that file until its claims are released and tells it to go ahead: at once if the agent is idle, else when its turn ends (Allow is often clicked while the blocked agent is still replying; the edit is then allowed again for the turn the go-ahead starts, and several files allowed in one turn get one go-ahead). The go-ahead is a delivery (#649: a control notice for that launch), offered as every line of Hive's is: only while the user isn't writing in that terminal (**Pause after you type**), checked again until Enter, so it is never added to or sends their input; put off, it goes when the pause ends, or at the end of the turn the user's own prompt started (the file stays allowed for it). An edit of the file that goes ahead meanwhile (in the same turn, or after the holder finished) needs no go-ahead, and an interrupt drops it, even one that comes while the go-ahead is being typed (it stops before Enter). A claim is released when the holder's turn ends (`Stop`), when its session ends, or 15 minutes after its last edit. Claims, approvals and questions are numbered as they happen, and a turn's end releases only those from before it arrived, so a late `Stop` never clears what the next turn has claimed (an agent's hooks are handled in order, but `PreToolUse` is answered at once). Denials return at once, so agents can't deadlock; if Hive doesn't answer, the edit goes ahead. Only these tools are covered: shell commands, formatters and Claude Code's own config writes (`~/.claude.json`) are not — concurrency there is the developer's responsibility. Agents in different worktrees never collide (different paths). Claimed files are shown on the agent's tab and pane header (lock icon with a count; the list on hover).

**Status line** (Claude Code): `launch/settings.json` also sets Claude Code's `statusLine` to the same `curl.exe` command, posting to `/hook?statusline`. The hook server answers 204 with no body, so Claude Code shows no status line in the terminal. From the JSON Hive takes the session's effort (`effort.level`), model name and cost (`cost.total_cost_usd`) into the live state, and the plan limits (`rate_limits.five_hour` / `seven_day`: `used_percentage`, `resets_at`) into `config.json` (`planUsage`, account-wide, last report wins). Claude Code reports with every status-line update, many times a minute while agents work, so the latest report is kept in memory and written only when a shown value changes (a whole percentage or a reset time), at most once a minute, and on quit. Warnings at 80% and 95% are shown once per limit per reset period (`planWarnings`; a reset time within 30 minutes of the stored one counts as the same period). Hive never calls Anthropic's usage API or reads credentials itself.

### 6.4 Codex

Tested with Codex 0.159 and 0.160. Hive never edits Codex's own `config.toml`; everything is passed as `-c key=value` overrides (TOML values):

```
codex [resume <id>] --no-daemon          # options must follow "resume <id>"
      -c hooks.<Event>=[…] -c hooks.state={…trusted hashes…}
      -c tui.terminal_title=["activity","project-name"]   # keeps "Action Required" in the title (Status)
      [-m <model>] [-c model_reasoning_effort=<e>] <preset flags (§5)>
      -c developer_instructions="<the hive MCP server's instructions>"
      -c mcp_servers.<name>={…}          # each enabled workspace server
      -c mcp_servers.<other>.enabled=false   # the user's and the project's own servers
      [extra args]
```

- **Hooks**: SessionStart, UserPromptSubmit, PreToolUse, PostToolUse, PermissionRequest, Stop, Interrupt, PreCompact, PostCompact, SessionEnd, each a command hook. Codex runs hook commands through PowerShell on Windows, so the command is `curl.exe -s -m 5 -X POST -H "@<auth file>" -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@-" "<url>"` (the launch's auth file, #345, so the `-c` arguments, which process listings show, carry no token). Codex only runs hooks it trusts: Hive passes the trust records in one `hooks.state` table, keyed by `<session-flags config path>:<event label>:0:0` with `sha256:` of the hook's canonical JSON. For each new Codex version Hive checks its hashes against Codex's own (the app server's `hooks/list`) once, and uses Codex's if they differ.
- **Ready**: Codex fires SessionStart only with the first prompt, so Hive marks the agent ready when Codex's prompt appears in the terminal, and records the session (its ID and rollout path) at the first hook.
- **Status**: who is asked comes from Codex's terminal title, not its hooks. Codex sends PermissionRequest before anyone answers it, in every preset with the same payload: in **Approve for me** its auto-reviewer (a Guardian sub-session that sends no hooks) answers it, allowing it (PostToolUse follows) or denying it (nothing follows). It titles its terminal `[ ! ] Action Required | <project>` (blinking with `[ . ]`) exactly while a person must act: an approval prompt, or a question. So from Codex 0.160.0 (`titleAttention()`; Hive pins the title's items so a user's `[tui].terminal_title` can't hide it) the title saying so makes the agent **waiting** (a permission request or `request_user_input`, which stops Codex), or gives it a **pending question** (`request_user_input_async`, which returns at once: Codex works on, and the answer comes later as a prompt). The hooks only say what is asked: PermissionRequest is the status message ("Codex asks to run …"), or, in **Approve for me**, an action under automatic review (`LiveSessionState.review`) while the reviewer considers it: the status stays **Working…** with a small shield beside it in the agent's and the Assistant's headers (`ReviewMark`), whose tooltip names the reviewer and what is asked; the request is never the status, a label elsewhere or a notification, and the Agent API reports it as `reviewing`. It goes when the next tool starts or ends, a prompt comes, the turn ends or is interrupted, the session ends, or a person is asked after all (no review is shown over a wait). The command in a request is its first line, cut at 120 characters with an ellipsis. The title is one for everything asked, so Hive keeps what is open: each ask from its hook until it is resolved (its own tool call ends, the turn ends or is interrupted, or the title goes back). The title coming on is about what is open (the latest prompt is waited on, the latest question shows beside it), else about something the hooks didn't say (waiting, with no message). A permission request in Approve for me is never open: the reviewer decides it (one it can't decide fails; one it handed to you would still be told, unnamed), so a finished review can't be taken for a later title. While the title is already on, an ask is judged by what it is: a reviewed request stays the reviewer's (the agent works on, a question stays pending, no alert); anything else is put to you at once, as a new need. A wait ends when the tool call it is about ends (its PostToolUse names the same tool and command as the request); another call ending beside it doesn't, and with two prompts up the wait moves on to the other. The title going back ends the wait and the question. A title change is applied in order with the hooks: Hive answers hooks at once and handles them one at a time, so a change waits for the hooks that came before it (a slow first hook, recording the session, could otherwise let the title land before that turn's prompt and its request, and the prompt be told twice). Older versions go by the hooks: PermissionRequest is **waiting** except in Approve for me (the preset's `reviewed`), a question waits or is pending, and the next prompt answers a pending one. Interrupt (Esc) means **ready** and releases the agent's file locks. File locks use PreToolUse with `apply_patch` (paths from the patch; in code mode too).
- **Live preset changes** go through Codex's `/permissions` menu: Hive clears the input, types `/permissions`, then the number its menu shows beside the preset's **label** (Read Only, Ask for approval, Approve for me, Full Access), read from Codex's screen as rendered (#396: Codex 0.161 reordered the menu from 0.160's Read Only, Ask, Approve, Full Access to Ask, Approve, Full Access, Read Only, so a fixed number picked the wrong preset; the arrow keys wrap around, so counting presses did too). A key step can be such a pick (`KeyStep` `pick`, `main/keySteps.ts`; the label match in `providers/codex/permissionsMenu.ts`): it waits up to 4 seconds for the menu, and a label that never shows fails clearly (the menu closed with Escape, nothing chosen: "Hive couldn't find …") rather than choosing another preset. Codex sessions keep a rendered screen for it. Each key goes in only while the session is still the one being switched, isn't asking the user anything and isn't busy (working, or Codex's screen showing it holding what was typed: "tab to queue message" under its input, `adapter.busyScreen`); busy, it waits, at most 30 seconds. Otherwise typing stops there (`KeysStopped`): nothing more goes in, the badge goes back, the mode stays as it was, and Hive says why ("…, as its session ended"). The Enter after `/permissions` goes in again while Codex's input still holds the command once it is free (#363, as for the sandbox setup below). The badge shows "Switching to X…" until Codex confirms, from its "Permission selection requested: X" line or the rollout's `thread_settings_applied`, within 12 seconds; otherwise it reverts and says so.
- **Models** come from Codex itself (`codex debug models`: the listed models, in Codex's order, with each one's reasoning levels and default) when the CLI is found, so new models appear without a Hive update (§10, models and capabilities); `model_reasoning_effort` in its config.toml is the default effort for every model when set.
- **Live details** (model, effort, context, plan limits, preset, Plan mode) come from the tail of the rollout file (`token_count` events and `thread_settings_applied`), read as it grows.
- **Sandbox**: on Windows Codex needs its sandbox set up once (`windows.sandbox` in its config); without it every command asks for approval. Agent Setup runs Codex read-only in a scratch folder and brings up Codex's own setup prompt (default or non-admin sandbox): with no sandbox yet, by choosing "Ask for approval" in `/permissions` (by its label, as live changes do; if Codex doesn't show it, a warning says so and the terminal stays open for the user); with the non-admin sandbox on (`unelevated`), by typing `/setup-default-sandbox`, which only exists then. The keys go in once Codex has loaded (`CommandSpec.readyPattern`, read from the screen its output draws, `KeyGate`: its session header names the folder where it said "loading", which Codex writes over in place; its prompt is drawn while it is still loading) and it has shown no sign of being busy for a second: no spinner in its window title (`CommandSpec.busyTitle`), and no "tab to queue message" on the last line it draws, under its input (`CommandSpec.busyScreen`, `codexHoldsInput()`; the same words in the conversation don't count), which can show before the title's spinner on a loaded machine (#363). Codex is briefly busy once it has loaded. Each key waits again until it has been free for half a second, asked just before it goes in; still busy after 30 seconds (the 30-second start too, for a Codex never seen loaded), or closed, typing stops there with a warning to finish in the terminal. The Enter that runs a command is then checked against Codex's screen (`KeyStep.heldOn`, `inputHolds()`): if, once Codex is free, has had a second to draw what it did, and is still free, its input (the last line it marks with "›") still holds just the command, unrun, Enter goes in again, at most twice more. A command Codex took (run, or queued by Codex to run when it is free) leaves its input, so it is never sent twice, and nothing is sent again on a guess (an Enter in an open menu would choose from it). Agent Setup explains the two choices in Hive's terms (`ReadinessIssue.detail`), and with the non-admin sandbox shows an info item with **Upgrade** (the same task). Codex stays open after its setup, so the task ends itself once Codex's config names a new sandbox (`CommandSpec.done`); Agent Setup then shows that the task succeeded (the issue it was started for is gone) instead of the terminal.
- **Resume**: `codex resume <id>` restores a rollout only at its original path, so Hive keeps that path in `sessions.json` and restores a backup there when Codex's copy is gone.
- `.git` is read-only in Codex's workspace-write sandbox (committing asks for approval).
- **A stray `.codex` folder** (#509): Codex treats a project's `.codex` as its own and protects it before every sandboxed command; one an agent wrote scratch into (made by the sandbox's own user) made that protection fail ("deny ACE failed on …\.codex"), so every Codex agent's commands in the project failed ("setup refresh had errors") until it was deleted. With Codex turned on, Hive warns (a toast, once while the folder stays so) when a folder an agent works in has a `.codex` holding anything that isn't Codex's configuration: `config.toml`, custom agents (`agents/*.toml`), `rules`, `prompts`, `skills`, and what those TOML files name in a path field (a key ending in `file`, `path` or `dir`, such as `model_instructions_file` or a skill's `path`: the file and the folders holding it; other text, such as a profile's name, names nothing) (`strayCodexEntries`); checked as each agent starts (any provider) and at each turn's end (`folderWarning` in the adapter). Hive's session contract tells agents to keep scratch, probes and logs in a temp folder, never in or beside a project or worktree, nor in its `.codex`, `.claude` or `.agents`.

---

### 6.5 Hive Assistant

- **What it is.** Each open workspace has one Assistant, its overseer, shown in a panel on the right of the window (Ctrl+Alt+I, the command palette, or clicking the strip it folds into when hidden: a narrow bar down the right edge with a chevron, "Hive Assistant" written downwards and a status dot while it runs; each workspace remembers whether its panel is open and how wide it is, 430 px by default). It answers questions about any project, reports what the agents are doing, plans and reviews, and (as far as **Control** allows, below) runs the agents for the user: adds them, changes their settings, starts, stops and briefs them, and creates projects. It never edits project files itself.
- **How it runs.** It is a session like an agent's (terminal, hooks, status, usage, transcript backups, quit/close/switch handling, notifications), hosted by `<Workspace>/.hive/assistant`, a home Hive never lists as a project, with one agent (`assistant`). It **works in the workspace folder**, so it can read every project, and gets the hive MCP server scoped to the workspace (no project of its own) and the workspace's skills meant for it (`metadata.audience` `assistant` or `all`, §7.1: `coordinate-agents`, `run-lanes`, `split-work`, `handover`, `pick-up`, `workspace-note` as Hive ships them), not the project agents' skills or the workspace's other MCP servers. Reading a skill grants nothing: its tools and Control decide what it can do. Quit and close dialogs call it "Assistant".
